What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. My primary care doctor suggested I come see you because I've been having these big eating spells lately. After some of these big eating spells, I just feel disgusted with myself and very guilty about what I’ve done.\n\nDoctor: During these big eating spells, do you ever try to compensate for what you've eaten by vomiting, using laxatives, or excessively exercising afterwards?\n\nPatient: No, doctor, I don't do any of those things at all. I usually just sit there at the kitchen table feeling absolutely terrible and ashamed of myself.\n\nDoctor: How often do you experience these big eating spells, and do you feel like you're able to control what you're eating or stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: These spells have been happening at least once or twice a week ever since my husband first went into the hospital in the spring. Once I start eating, I completely lose control; it feels like something takes over my hands and I just can't stop. Even when I am telling myself, \"Maria, stop, you don't need more,\" I keep scooping food onto my plate like I can't hit the brakes.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes of eating, do you feel like you're eating an amount of food that's definitely larger than what most people would eat under similar circumstances within a short period of time, and is your weight or body image a significant concern for you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I definitely do—I’ll eat what my whole family would normally finish in a Sunday dinner, like a whole tray of chicken or a big pot of rice and stew, all by myself in one evening. The shame is that I completely lose control and then sit there calling myself a pig in the quiet of the night. Even when I am telling myself, \"Maria, stop, you don't need more,\" I keep scooping food onto my plate like I can't hit the brakes.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes of eating, do you feel like you're eating an amount of food that's definitely larger than what most people would eat under similar circumstances within a short period of time, and is your weight or body image a significant concern for you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I definitely do—I’ll eat what my whole family would normally finish in a Sunday dinner, like a whole tray of chicken or a big pot of rice and stew, all by myself in one evening. As for my weight, that isn't really my main concern; it's the shame of losing control and the behavior itself that makes me feel so terrible.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by 'the shame of losing control' and how that relates to your body image, if at all?\n\nPatient: It really isn't about my body image or how I look at all, doctor. The shame is that I completely lose control and then sit there calling myself a pig in the quiet of the night. I just feel so much guilt for behaving this way in secret when I need to be strong and focused on taking care of my husband.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your weight or body image isn't a significant concern for you, but can you tell me if you have any fears about gaining weight or becoming overweight as a result of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I haven't really thought much about gaining weight, to be honest. My mind is just so occupied with the guilt and shame of losing control like that. It's the behavior itself and how it makes me feel inside that is my main concern, not my weight.\n\nDoctor: Just to clarify, you've mentioned that your weight or body image isn't a significant concern, but I want to make sure: do you have any concerns at all about how your body might change as a result of these eating episodes, even if it's not the main issue for you?\n\nPatient: I haven't really thought about my weight or my body shape changing, to be honest. My main worries are the intense guilt, feeling disgusted with myself, and how physically heavy and sluggish I feel afterward. It's really not about how my body looks."

It's really not about how my body looks.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant stressors or emotional events in your life, aside from your husband's hospitalization, that you think might be contributing to these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yes, besides the hospitalizations, the daily grind of being a full-time caregiver—managing my husband's meds, appointments, and chores—has been incredibly exhausting along with my own aging-related fatigue. I also had to stop volunteering at the community center and singing in the choir, which has left me feeling so isolated and without my usual social outlets. It has just been a very lonely and stressful time."

    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hello, doctor. My primary care doctor suggested I come see you because I've been having these big eating spells lately. After some of these big eating spells, I just feel disgusted with myself and very guilty about what I’ve done.\n\nDoctor: During these big eating spells, do you ever try to compensate for what you've eaten by vomiting, using laxatives, or excessively exercising afterwards?\n\nPatient: No, doctor, I don't do any of those things at all. I usually just sit there at the kitchen table feeling absolutely terrible and ashamed of myself.\n\nDoctor: How often do you experience these big eating spells, and do you feel like you're able to control what you're eating or stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: These spells have been happening at least once or twice a week ever since my husband first went into the hospital in the spring. Once I start eating, I completely lose control; it feels like something takes over my hands and I just can't stop. Even when I am telling myself, \"Maria, stop, you don't need more,\" I keep scooping food onto my plate like I can't hit the brakes.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes of eating, do you feel like you're eating an amount of food that's definitely larger than what most people would eat under similar circumstances within a short period of time, and is your weight or body image a significant concern for you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I definitely do—I’ll eat what my whole family would normally finish in a Sunday dinner, like a whole tray of chicken or a big pot of rice and stew, all by myself in one evening. As for my weight, that isn't really my main concern; it's the shame of losing control and the behavior itself that makes me feel so terrible.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about what you mean by 'the shame of losing control' and how that relates to your body image, if at all?\n\nPatient: It really isn't about my body image or how I look at all, doctor. The shame is that I completely lose control and then sit there calling myself a pig in the quiet of the night. I just feel so much guilt for behaving this way in secret when I need to be strong and focused on taking care of my husband.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your weight or body image isn't a significant concern for you, but can you tell me if you have any fears about gaining weight or becoming overweight as a result of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I haven't really thought much about gaining weight, to be honest. My mind is just so occupied with the guilt and shame of losing control like that. It's the behavior itself and how it makes me feel inside that is my main concern, not my weight.\n\nDoctor: Just to clarify, you've mentioned that your weight or body image isn't a significant concern, but I want to make sure: do you have any concerns at all about how your body might change as a result of these eating episodes, even if it's not the main issue for you?\n\nPatient: I haven't really thought about my weight or my body shape changing, to be honest. My main worries are the intense guilt, feeling disgusted with myself, and how physically heavy and sluggish I feel afterward. It's really not about how my body looks.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant stressors or emotional events in your life, aside from your husband's hospitalization, that you think might be contributing to these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Yes, besides the hospitalizations, the daily grind of being a full-time caregiver—managing my husband's meds, appointments, and chores—has been incredibly exhausting along with my own aging-related fatigue. I also had to stop volunteering at the community center and singing in the choir, which has left me feeling so isolated and without my usual social outlets. It has just been a very lonely and stressful time.\n\nDoctor: You've mentioned that your weight and body image aren't significant concerns for you, but can you tell me if you've ever had any past struggles with body image or disordered eating that might be relevant to what you're experiencing now?\n\nPatient: No, doctor, I've never really struggled with my weight, body image, or my eating habits in the past. This is a completely new experience for me that has only started with the recent stress of caring for my husband.\n\nDoctor: You've consistently mentioned that your weight and body image are not significant concerns for you, but I want to clarify one more time: are you certain that the guilt and shame you feel after these eating episodes are not, in any way, related to fears about gaining weight or changing your body shape?"
  }

I just feel like I'm failing both my husband and myself during this very stressful time, and that is what hurts so much.

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. After some of these big eating spells, I just feel disgusted with myself and very guilty about what I’ve done.\n\nDoctor: During these big eating spells, do you ever try to compensate for what you've eaten by vomiting, using laxatives, or excessively exercising afterwards?\n\nPatient: No, doctor, I don't do any of those things at all. I usually just sit there at the kitchen table feeling absolutely terrible and ashamed of myself.\n\nDoctor: How often do you experience these big eating spells, and do you feel like you're able to control what you're eating or stop once you've started?\n\nPatient: These spells have been happening at least once or twice a week ever since my husband first went into the hospital in the spring. Once I start eating, I completely lose control; it feels like something takes over my hands and I just can't stop. Even when I am telling myself, \"Maria, stop, you don't need more,\" I keep scooping food onto my plate like I can't hit the brakes.\n\nDoctor: During these episodes of eating, do you feel like you're eating an amount of food that's definitely larger than what most people would eat under similar circumstances within a short period of time, and is your weight or body image a significant concern for you?\n\nPatient: Yes, I definitely do—I’ll eat what my whole family would normally finish in a Sunday dinner, like a whole tray of chicken or a big pot of rice and stew, all by myself in one evening. The guilt and shame come entirely from losing control and eating in secret when I feel I should be strong. I just feel like I'm failing both my husband and myself during this very stressful time, and that is what hurts so much.\n\nDoctor: Is it possible that your feelings of guilt and shame after eating are indirectly related to a fear of losing your role or identity, rather than specifically about your body image or weight?"

Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
